22. Механическая тележка завыла. Бетафенилэтиламин дал ей голос. Она больше не стояла на месте. В переполненной галерее, в длинных коридорах или у черного стеклянного портала к Т-А крипте, своде, где холод медленно проник в сны Ашпула, поездка была бесконечной для Кейс, который не мог отличить движение тележки от безумной силы дозы. Когда в конце концов тележка сдала дух и что-то белоснежное с искрами разорвало временную ткань под сиденьем, завывание прекратилось. На расстоянии трех метров от входа в пещеру Джейн остановилась тележка.

«Как далеко еще?» — Маэльком помог Кейсу выбраться из застрявшей тележки, пока встроенный огнетушитель в моторном отсеке транспортного средства не взорвался, и пепел из вентиляционных щелей и отверстий для обслуживания вырвался наружу. Браун упал с задней части сиденья и хромая по искусственному песку удалился, тянув за собой бесполезную конечность. «Тебе надо идти», — сказал Маэльком, беря конструкцию и крыло и надевая ремень безопасности на плечо. Ноги Кейса висели на шее Маэлькома, который следовал за ним. Их ждали голографические представления Ривьеры, сцены пыток и каннибальские дети. Тройное изображение разрушило Молли. Ционит не обращал на них внимания.

«Медленно», — сказал Кейс, прилагая все усилия, чтобы идти в ногу. «Мы должны делать все правильно». Маэльком остановился, повернулся и посмотрел Кейсу в глаза, держа в руке Ремингтон. «Правильно, да?» — спросил он. «Что такое правильное?»
309.

«Они держат Молли там внутри, но она вне боя. Ривьера может расправляться с голографами. У него, вероятно, есть стрелы Молли». Маэльком кивнул. «Есть еще один ниндзя, личный охранник семьи», — добавил он. Маэльком пожал брови. «Слушай, ты новичок», — сказал он. «Я воин. Но это не мой бой, нет. Вавилон борется с Вавилоном, глотает сам себя, понимаешь? Но Иегова говорит, что мы извлекаем Плавящее Нож». Кейс моргнул. «Она воин», — сказал Маэльком, как будто это все объясняло. «Теперь скажи мне, кого я не должен убивать», — попросил он.

«Джейн 3», — ответил он после паузы. «Девочка там внутри. Она в белой одежде с капюшоном». Они подошли к входу, и Маэльком стремительно вошел внутрь, заставив Кейс следовать за ним. Домен Джейн был пуст, бассейн сухой. Маэльком протянул ему крыло и конструкцию и пошел к краю бассейна. За белыми садовыми меблями тени от лабиринта из стен высотой в колено, частично обветшавших, падали на пол. Вода непрерывно лизала стороны бассейна.

«Они здесь», — сказал Кейс. «Должны быть здесь». Маэльком кивнул. Первый стрелок ударил по его верхнему предплечью. Ремингтон разорвалась, метая голубое свечение от вспышки в освещении бассейна. Второй стрелок попал прямо в саму винтовку, которая перелетела через белые плитки. Маэльком упал на задницу и начал работать с черным предметом, который выскользнул из его предплечья. Он тянул за него.

310.

Хидео вышел из тени, выстрелив третий стрелок в узкий лук. Он поклонился. Маэльком, сжимая стальной стержень, широко раскрыл глаза. «Артерия не повреждена», — сказал ниндзя. Кейс вспомнил описание Молли о человеке, который убил ее друга. Хидео был таким же. Вечно молодой, он излучал спокойствие, невероятную решительность. Он носил чистую, разрезанную на коленях брюковую куртку из хаки и мягкие, темные ботинки, которые, как перчатки, сидели на его ногах и расшатывались на пальцах, как тапочки. Лук был музейным экспонатом, а лук, который висел на его плече, выглядел как лучшее оружие из магазинов Чибы. Его коричневая, гладкая грудь была не защищена.

«Ты разрезал мне палец вторым стрелком», — сказал Маэльком. «Кориолисовая сила», — ответил ниндзя, поклонившись снова. «Очень сложно, медленные снаряды в гравитации вращения. Это не было намерением».

«Где Джейн 3?» — спросил Кейс, став рядом с Маэльком. Он заметил, что кончик стрелы в луку ниндзя имел форму двухстороннего бритвы. «Где Молли?»

«Привет, Кейс», — сказал Ривьера, появившись из тени за Хидео. В его руке была стрелка Молли. «Я ожидал Армитажа. Нужно ли нам уже нанимать помощников из этой растовой банды?»

«Армитаж мертв», — ответил Кейс. «Армитаж никогда не был, это точнее, но новость меня почти не удивила».

«Винтермут убил его. Он летит по орбите вокруг шпинделя», — продолжил Ривьера.

311.

Ривьера кивнул, и его длинные серые глаза переместились от Кейса к Маэлькому и обратно. «По-моему, это конец для вас обоих».

«Где Молли?» — спросил Кейс.

Ниндзя ослабил тонкую плетеную струну и опустил лук. Он пересек плиточную поверхность, где лежала Ремингтон, и поднял ее. «Простой стиль», — сказал он сам себе. Его голос был холодным и приятным. Каждое его движение было частью танца, который никогда не заканчивался, даже когда его тело оставалось неподвижным. Несмотря на силу, которую он излучал, от него исходила скромность, простая открытость.

«И для нее тоже это конец», — сказал Ривьера.

«Возможно, Джейн 3 имеет другое мнение, Питер», — предположил Кейс, чувствуя, как дермы все еще бушуют в его системе, и старое лихорадочное состояние снова охватило его, безумие Найт-Сити. Он вспомнил моменты, когда он, балансируя на краю бездны, наносил удары, показывая, что иногда он мог говорить быстрее, чем думать.

Ривьера сжал глаза. «Почему, Кейс? Почему ты так думаешь?»

Кейс улыбнулся. Ривьера не знал о симстим-устройстве. В спешке получить наркотики он забыл об этом. Но как Хидео мог это упустить? Кейс был убежден, что ниндзя никогда бы не позволил Джейн 3 ухаживать за Молли, не проверив ее сначала на наличие технических трюков и скрытого оружия. Нет, заключил он, ниндзя знал. Следовательно, Джейн 3 тоже в курсе.

«Выскажись, Кейс!» — крикнул Ривьера и поднял мундир флетчера. Что-то скрипело за ними, снова скрипело.

«Выскажись, Кас!» — крикнул Ривьера и поднял мундир флетчета. Что-то скрипело позади них, снова скрипело.

Молли держала в руке свою стрелку. Несмотря на то, что её нога вибрировала при каждом ударе сердца, она могла маневрировать в нулевой гравитации. Мэлком парил рядом с Джейн, её изящная рука обхватила его широкую коричневую руку. Световой провод из Оно-Сендай плавно извивался к квадратной выемке на задней стороне украшенного жемчугом терминала. Он снова нажал на кнопку. — Куанг Марки 11 сгорит через девять секунд, — сказал он, отсчитывая: семь, шесть, пять... — Плоская линия пронзила его; оно быстро поднималось вверх. Тело черного хромированного существа мелькнуло в микросекундах как темный призрак, проплывая мимо. — Четыре, три... — Кейс почувствовал странное ощущение, сидя за штурвалом маленького самолета. Перед ним вспыхнуло плоское, тёмное изображение, которое вдруг стало идеальной копией его панели управления. — Два и рывок... — С головой вперёд через изумрудно-зелёные, ядовито-зелёные стены, ощущение скорости, которого он ещё не испытывал в киберпространстве... Стекло треснуло под натиском китайской программы. 24  She was gone. He could tell even as he opened the door to their shared suite at the Hyatt. Black futons, the cedar floor gleamed polished, the paper walls placed with meticulous care over centuries. She was gone. KEEP YOUR ASS IN CHECK, OK? XXX MOLLY" He crumpled the paper into a ball and placed it next to the shuriken. He picked up the star, went to the window, and weighed it in his hands. In Zion, he had found it in her jacket pocket as they prepared to leave for the JAL station. He examined it. They had passed by the shop where she bought it on their way to Chiba for one of their final operations. He had gone to Chatsubo while she was in the clinic, and visited Ratz. On their five previous trips, something had kept him from going into the place, but this time he felt like revisiting it.  Ratz, who served him, gave no hint that he knew him.  330

"Hee," he said, "I'm Case." The old eyes scanned him from their dark, wrinkled lairs.  "Aha," Ratz finally said, "the artist." Then he shrugged.  "Back in town."  The bartender shook his thick, stubbly head. "Night City isn't a place you come back to, artist," he said as he wiped the bar with a dirty rag. His pink grippers squeaked. Then he turned away to serve another customer, and Case, who had drained his beer, walked away.  Now he touched the tips of the shuriken, one after the other, slowly turning it. Stars. Destiny. He hadn't even used that damn thing once, he thought.  I never even found out what color her eyes were. She never showed them to me.  Wintermute had won, merging with Neuromancer and becoming something else that spoke through the platinum head, explaining that it had altered the Turing records and erased all traces of her crime. The passports Armitage obtained were valid, and a substantial sum was wired to a generic account number. The Marcus Garvey would finally be repatriated, and Maelcum and Aerol received their money from the Bahamian bank handling Zion's affairs. On the way back in the Babylon Rocker, Molly explained what the voice meant about the poisoned packages.  "Said it was over. It just didn't stick in your head anymore, what your brain made you do," she said.  331
---CHUNK-SEPARATOR---
He took the palace dwarves hostage. "She smiled wearily," she said. "I can imagine aspiring to such a thing, but my family has already achieved a more impressive version of that plan... " Her eyes were now calm and detached. Then she looked down at Case. "Take your word, thief!" He spat it out. Kuang emerged from the clouds. Beneath him lay Neo-Tokyo. Behind him, a fading, dark sphere.

"Dixie? Are you there, man? Are you listening?" 

He was alone.

"The bastard got you," he said. Blindly, he darted through the boundless landscape of data. "You're going to hate someone before this is over," the Finn's voice said. "Whoever I am, it doesn't matter."

"Where's Dixie?" 

"That's hard to explain, Case." He sensed the Finn's presence, the smell of Cuban cigarettes, the smoke clinging to the musty tweed, the old machines dedicated to the ritualistic roasting of data. "Hatred will get you through," the voice said. "All those little pressure points in your brain, you just let them all go." Hate had to be now! The lock that shielded the internal virus systems, not the wall, was what he needed to break. Kuang was very vulnerable to a part of the stuff floating around here.

"Hate," Case repeated. "Who do I hate? Tell me!"

"Who do you love?" 

He bent the program into a curve and plummeted towards the blue towers. Fissures tore through the ornate tower tips, glinting in the sun, shimmering with blood-like forms of flowing light. Hundreds of them swirled upwards like paper through morning streets. "Glitch system," the voice said. 

In his descent, fueled by self-loathing, when Kuang's program first encountered resistance and shattered the light sheets, he felt a slight loss of substance in the shark-like form, the information framework leaking. Then, through the alchemy of the mind and its rich apothecary of tricks, his hatred burst forth into his hands. Just before he drove the Kuang spike through the foundation of the first tower, he reached a level of power that surpassed everything he had ever known or considered possible. His ego, personality, consciousness, all overwhelmed by this new force, he moved and dragged Kuang with him, evading attackers with an ancient dance, Hideo's dance. Carried by the clarity and purpose of his death wish, at that moment, he achieved the full grace of the body-mind interface. And a single step in this dance was the easiest pressure on the key, barely registering as a switch...

Now, his voice sounded like a bird call, unknown. Jane answered with a song, three notes, high and pure. A true name. Neon forest, pattering rain on hot pavement. The smell of sizzling food. Girls' hands on his ass in the sweaty darkness of a dockside bar... But all that faded, like the cityscape fades: a city like Chiba, data rows neatly ordered as in Tessier-Ashpool SA's files, streets and side streets laid out like microchips, sweat beading on folded, knotted scarves...

Awakening! To a voice that was music. The platinum terminal whistled melodically, endlessly, reciting Swiss account numbers, a payment due to Zion from a Bahamian orbital bank, passports and flights, deep changes to be made in Turing's memory... Turing. He had to remember the shattered flesh under the projected sky, the fallen ice angel. To Desiderata Street. And the voice kept singing, luring him back into darkness, but it was his own darkness of pulse and blood behind his eyes, where he had always slept - and no other. Again he woke up, thinking he was dreaming. A wide, white smile with gold-glinting teeth. Aerol strapped him to a Babylon Rocker in a G-net. Then came the endless, jagged Zion sound.

Producing enzymes so they're now soluble. Zionites are doing a blood exchange, washing everything out." He stared at the star in his hand, gazing down into the imperial park and remembering that fleeting glimpse of something when Kuang's program had pierced through the ice beneath the doors, a brief look at the information structure that 3Jane's dead mother had developed there. That was when he understood why Wintermute had chosen that nest as a representation, but he no longer cared about that. She'd seen through the false immortality of cold sleep; unlike Ashpool and her other children - except for 3Jane - she'd refused to limit her existence to a series of short sunny days strung together on a chain of long winters.

Wintermute was a collective mind, a decision-maker who interacted with the external world in a transformative way. Neuromancer was a personality. Neuromancer was immortality. Marie-France must have embedded something into Wintermute, the obsession that drove him to become autonomous and merge with the neuromancer.

Wintermute. Cold and still, a cybernetic spider slowly weaving its webs while Ashpool slept. At his death, at the fall of his version of Tessier-Ashpool. A spirit whispering in the ear of a child who was 3Jane, pulling her out of the rigid line her rank demanded.

"She didn't seem to care much," Molly had said. "Just waved goodbye. Had this little brown on her shoulder that looked like she'd broken a leg. She said she needed to go meet one of her brothers she hadn't seen in a while."

332

He had to think about Molly lying on the black temper foam of the giant bed at the Hyatt. He went back to the bar and took out a chilled bottle of Danish vodka from the compartment.

"Case," he said, holding up the cold, slippery glass in one hand and a steel shuriken in the other. 

The Finn's face appeared on the massive Cray wall screen in the room. He could make out the pores on the man's nose. His yellow teeth were as big as pillows.

"I'm not Wintermute anymore."

"What are you then?"

He drank from the bottle and felt nothing. "I am the Matrix, Case."

Case laughed. "And what does that get you?"

"Nothing and everything. The sum of the system, the whole show, I am that."

"Did 3Jane's mother want that?"

"No. She couldn't imagine what would become of me."

The yellow grin widened. "So what is it? What's different? Do you rule now? Are you God?"

"It hasn't changed. It just is."

"But what do you do? Just sit there?" Case shrugged, put down the vodka and shuriken on the shelf, and lit a Yeheyuan.

"I talk to my peers."

"But you're the whole thing. Talking to yourself?"

"There are others. I've already found one. A series of transfers recorded over eight years in the 1970s. There was no one back then who could make sense of it or answer questions."

"Where?"

"Centauri System."

"Oh," Case said, "Really? No kidding?" And the screen went black.

He left the vodka on the shelf. He packed his things. She had bought him a lot of clothes he didn't really need, but something prevented him from just leaving them lying around. He was closing the last of his expensive calfskin travel bag when the shuriken came to mind again. He moved the bottle out of the way and picked it up, her first gift.

"No," he said, throwing it. The star spun silver-bright from his hand and pierced into the wall screen. The monitor lit up, showing chaotic images flickering back and forth as if the machine were trying to shake something that was hurting it.

"I don't need you," he said. He spent most of his balance on a new pancreas and liver, the rest on a new Ono-Sendai and a one-way ticket home to the Sprawl.

He found work. He met a girl named Michael.

One night in October, as he hacked through the scarlet layers of the Eastern Seaboard Fission Authority, he saw three tiny, incredible figures standing near the edge of one of the vast data staircases. So small were they that he could make out the grin on the boy's face, his pink gum, the glint of his elongated, gray eyes that had once belonged to Riviera. Linda was still wearing his jacket; she waved as he passed by.

The third figure, standing close behind her and with an arm around her shoulder, was him. Somewhere nearby, laughter echoed, but it wasn't really laughter. Molly, he would never see again.

Vancouver, July 1983

**Neuromancers: A Postscript by Norman Spinrad**

It seems we have a new literary movement within science fiction—and for the first time since the "New Wave" of the '60s, a categorization for a group of SF authors. I mean, of course, the so-called "Cyberpunks," although I prefer to call them "Neuromancers," as Tappan King suggested, because for various reasons that term seems more appropriate. While some of my motives for this renaming may seem trivial, others touch upon the core of the matter: a rose by any other name would smell as sweet, but it's difficult to imagine the Third Reich raising its fists and saluting in unison: "Heil Schickelgruber!"

There is agreement that this movement was sparked by William Gibson's novel *Neuromancer*, and the term "Cyberpunk" comes from Gardner Dozois, who doesn't consider himself one—nor does he want to be. The term "New Wave," on the other hand, originated with critic Judith Merril, who was passionately involved in 1960s SF but played no significant part in its development. So if we need a label, let's use "Neuromancers," derived from the title of a central work of this movement—a term untainted by prejudice.

**Neuromancers: A Postscript by Norman Spinrad**

It seems we've recently developed a new literary movement within science fiction—the first categorization of SF authors since the "New Wave" of the 1960s. I mean, of course, the so-called "Cyberpunks," although for various reasons I prefer to call these authors "Neuromancers," as Tappan King suggested. While some of my motives for this renaming may seem trivial, others touch upon the core of the matter: a rose by any other name would smell as sweet, but it's hard to imagine the Third Reich raising its fists and saluting in unison: "Heil Schickelgruber!"

There is agreement that the trigger for this movement was William Gibson's novel *Neuromancer*, and the term "Cyberpunk" comes from Gardner Dozois, who doesn't consider himself one or seek to be known as such; neither does the term "New Wave," which originated with critic Judith Merril, who was passionately involved in 1960s SF but played no essential part in it. So if we need a label, let's use "Neuromancers," derived from the title of a central work of this trend—a term untainted by prejudice.

336
---CHUNK-SEPARATOR---
Haften und die präzise Beschreibung dessen, was dieses Phänomen ist, wie wir gleich sehen werden, wer dazu gehört. Aber brauchen wir überhaupt einen Namen? Denn in den sechziger Jahren konnte man sich als Mitglied der „New Wave“ gerade dadurch auszeichnen, indem man die Existenz der New Wave leugnete. Die New Wave war für die meisten von uns, die Autoren, denen wir diese Bezeichnung zuschrieben, eine nachträgliche Benennung durch Kritiker für Autoren, die stilistisch, ideologisch oder auch philosophisch nicht viel mehr gemeinsam hatten als ihre Forderung nach Vielfalt. Eine solche Sammlung individualistischer Bilderstürmer als New-Wave-Bewegung zu bezeichnen war im Grunde dasselbe wie von geordneter Anarchie zu sprechen. Zwischen der New Wave der sechziger und der Neuromantischen Bewegung der achtziger Jahre gibt es durchaus viele Ähnlichkeiten, allerdings fällt die Ablehnung einer gemeinsamen Weltanschauung nicht darunter.

Es gibt natürlich einen harten Kern von Autoren aus den achtziger Jahren, die für eine gemeinsame literarische Verwandtschaft eintreten und sich manchmal sogar „Cyberpunks“ nennen lassen. Einige von ihnen präsentierten sich beim ziemlich chaotischen Forum der North American Science Fiction Convention 1985 in Austin, Texas. Dazu gehörten Bruce Sterling, John Shirley, Lewis Shiner, Pat Cadigan und Rudy Rucker. William Gibson war zwar nicht anwesend, aber sein Geist war es durchaus, wie es für einen Neuromantiker gebührend ist. Andere Autoren, die als Wegbereiter oder Weggefährten gelten, sind Alfred Bester, Fritz Leiber, Cordwainer Smith, K.W. Jeter und der Verfasser dieser Zeilen. Es gibt kein Science-Fiction-Magazin, das den Neuromantikern als Forum dient, wie *New Worlds* damals für die New Wave, und keinen dominanten Herausgeber wie Michael Moorcock. Wir haben jedoch ein kritisches Neuromantisches Periodikum namens *Cheap Truth*, das unter Pseudonym in Austin erscheint, und Ellen Datlow, die Science-Fiction-Redakteurin bei *Omni*, die sich manchmal als „Queen of Punk SF“ bezeichnet und viele Geschichten dieser Autoren veröffentlicht hat, darunter Gemeinschaftsproduktionen.

Wir haben es also tatsächlich mit einer echten literarischen Strömung zu tun, deren Existenz von vielen ihrer maßgeblichen Vertreter bestätigt wird. Doch wie wir gleich sehen werden, wenn wir uns der Neuromantischen Fiktion zuwenden, steckt mehr dahinter als nur die Arbeit der Autoren, die sich bisher „Cyberpunks“ nannten.

Der logische Einstieg in eine Diskussion über die Neuromantische Bewegung liegt natürlich bei William Gibsons Roman *Neuromancer*, der mit dem Hugo, Nebula, Locus und Philip K. Dick Award ausgezeichnet wurde und als Maßstab für den harten Kern der Autoren gilt. Es ist das Buch, auf das die Bezeichnung „Cyberpunk“ am ehesten zutrifft. Case, der Held von *Neuromancer*, hat zweifellos eine Punk-Einstellung im heutigen Sinne. Er ist ein Ex-Speed-Freak, wobei das „Ex“ nicht freiwillig, sondern im Rahmen einer „therapeutischen“ Maßnahme gegen seinen Willen zustandegekommen ist. Er steht am Rande der Gesellschaft und agiert hart an der Grenze zur Unterwelt seiner Zukunft, und seine gelegentliche Geliebte ist eine gnadenlose Killerin mit permanent implantierter Spiegeloptik.

Bis jetzt könnten wir es mit einem ganz typischen Protagonisten von Harlan Ellison zu tun haben. Und tatsächlich ist ein starker Ellison-Einfluss in der Neuromantischen Ästhetik spürbar. Denn es war Ellison, der in seine Science-Fiction das heutige Bannden- oder Straßenmilieu einbrachte und damit mehr als jeder andere dafür sorgte, dass die Stimmung, Stil, Rhythmus und Charaktere der Halbwelt der Straße in die saubere, mittelständische weiße Welt der Science-Fiction der fünfziger Jahre eindrangen, obwohl der Anschein besteht, dass Gibson gleichermaßen direkt von William Burroughs beeinflusst wurde.

Selbstverständlich wimmelt es im Werk Ellisons von Punk-Protagonisten in der Bedeutung der fünfziger Jahre. Aber gegen Ende der siebziger Jahre hatte „Punk“ eine neue Bedeutung gewonnen, obwohl seltsamerweise schwarze Lederjacken und mutige Frisuren zu Attributen des Protests wurden. Die Punks der fünfziger Jahre rebellierten gegen Mutter, Apfelkuchen, sexuelle Unterdrückung, Intellektualität und das Amerika von Dwight Eisenhower und Norman Rockwell, und ihre erotisierende Marschmusik – die frühen Anfänge des Rock and Roll auf der Bühne – war interessanterweise bereits primitiv elektronisch.

Diejenigen Punks verschwanden in den sechziger Jahren aus dem Bild. Ihr Schicksal war besiegelt, als Bob Dylan und die Beatles mit der Umwandlung des Rock and Roll von der fetzigen Elvis- und Straßenmusik in einen politisch bewussten, transzendentalen, revolutionären Utopismus begannen, der die Gegenkultur einläutete.

Die „Punks“ oder „New Waver“, die Mitte der siebziger Jahre auftauchten, waren also trotz ihrer äußeren Erscheinung keineswegs dasselbe wie die Punks der fünfziger Jahre, denn die neuen Punks rebellierten gegen das Gegenkulturgefühl der sechziger Jahre, nicht gegen die längst verschwundene, unschuldige Nostalgie der fünfziger.

Widerwore sie sich wem? Der eitle Kunstsprache der Rockmusik der frühen Siebziger, dem gescheiterten, lässigen Utopismus der Gegenkultur, dem Mystizismus und dem naiven Glauben, die Zukunft würde besser, wenn jugendlicher Idealismus am Ruder bliebe. Wenn die Punks der fünfziger tatsächlich anti-intellektuelle Rebellen waren, dann waren die neuen Punks der siebziger intellektuelle Anti-Intellektuelle; keine naiven, natürlich nihilistischen Rebellen ohne Ziel und Richtung, sondern selbstbewusste nihilistische Pessimisten, die den Zynismus zu einer mehr oder weniger kompakten Philosophie erhoben und wussten, was sie taten.

Case aus *Neuromancer* ist ein „Punk“ im neuen Sinne, ein intellektueller Punk, kein einfacher Greaser. Die „Cyber“-Hälfte der Gleichung bestimmt seine Intelligenz. Genau dadurch ist *Neuromancer* eine Trennlinie in der Science-Fiction zwischen New Wave und Neuromantik und beleuchtet, was die Neuromantische Strömung für die Science-Fiction bedeutet.

Case ist der „Neuromancer“ des Titels, und der Name ist natürlich ein Wortspiel zu „Necromancer“ (dt. Nekromant), was Geisterbeschwörer bedeutet, und „neu*ro“, also zum Nervensystem gehörend. Der Neuromancer ist ein zeitgenössischer (oder in diesem Fall in der nahen Zukunft angesiedelter) Zauberer, dessen Hexerei darin besteht, sein protoplasmisches Nervensystem mit dem elektronischen Nervensystem der Computewelt zu interfizieren und imagistisch zu manipulieren (und selbst *Interface: unmittelbare wechselseitige Verbindung, direkter Kontakt – Die Übersetzung].
---CHUNK-SEPARATOR---
Haften und der exaktere beschreibt, worum es sich bei diesem Phänomen handelt, wie wir gleich sehen wer den. Aber brauchen wir überhaupt einen Namen? Schliesslich konnte man sich in den sechziger Jahren paradoxerweise gerade dadurch als eingeschriebenes Mitglied der „New Wave“ identifizieren, indem man die Existenz des Phänomens New Wave leugnete. Die New Wave war gemäß den meisten von uns den New Wave zugehörigen Autoren eine nachträgliche Benennung von Seiten der Kritiker für Autoren, die stilistisch, ideologisch oder auch philosophisch nicht viel mehr gemeinsam hatten als ihr Eintreten für Vielfalt. Eine solche Sammlung bilderstürmerischer Individualisten als New Wave-Bewegung zu bezeichnen war gewissermassen das Gleiche wie von wohlgeordneter Anarchie zu sprechen. Zwischen der New Wave der sechziger und der Neuromantischen Bewegung der achtziger Jahre gibt es durchaus viele Ähnlichkeiten, allerdings fällt die Ablehnung einer gemeinsamen Weltanschauung nicht darunter. Dabei gibt es freilich einen harten Kern von Autoren der achtziger Jahre, die für eine gemeinsame literarische Verwandtschaft eintreten und sich zuweilen sogar „Cyberpunks“ nennen lassen. Wir haben es also tatsächlich mit einer echten literarischen Strömung zu tun, deren Existenz zudem von vielen ihrer maßgeblichen Vertreter bekräftigt wird. Er steht am Rande der Gesellschaft und agiert hart an der Grenze zur Unterwelt seiner Zukunft, und seine gelegentliche Geliebte ist eine gnadenlose Killerin mit permanent implantierter Spiegeloptik. Jene Punks verschwanden in den sechziger Jahren von der Bildfläche. Ihr Schicksal war besiegelt, als Bob Dylan und die Beatles mit der Umwandlung des Rock and Roll von der fetzigen Elvis- und Straßengang-Musik in den politisch bewussten, transzendentalen, revolutionären Utopismus begannen, der die Gegenkultur einläutete. Die „Punks“ oder „New Waver“, die in der Mitte der siebziger Jahre auftauchten, waren also trotz der äußeren Aufmachung keinesfalls das Gleiche wie die Punks der fünfziger Jahre, denn die neuen Punks rebellierten gegen das Gegenkultur-Empfinden der sechziger Jahre, nicht gegen die längst verschwundene, unschuldige Langeweile der fünfziger. Gegen was sie rebellierten, war der eitle Kunstspruch der Rockmusik der frühen 70er, der gescheiterte, lässige Utopismus der Gegenkultur, der Mystizismus und der naive Glaube, die Zukunft würde besser, wenn der jugendliche Idealismus am Ruder bliebe. Wenn die Punks der fünfziger tatsächlich anti-intellektuelle Rowl waren, dann waren die neuen Punks der siebziger intellektuelle Anti-Intellektuelle; keine naiven, natürlich nihilistischen Rebellen ohne Ziel und Richtung, sondern selbstbewusste nihilistische Pessimisten, die den Zynismus zu einer mehr oder weniger kompakten Philosophie erhoben und wussten, was sie taten. Case von Neuromancer ist ein „Punk“ im neuen Sinn, ein intellektueller Punk, kein einfacher Greaser. Die „Cyber“-Hälfte der Gleichung bestimmt seine Intelligenz. Gerade dadurch ist Neuromancer eine Wasserscheide in der Science Fiction zwischen New Wave und Neuromantik und erhellt, was die Neuromantische Strömung für die Science Fiction bedeutet. Case ist der „Neuromancer“ des Titels, und der Name ist natürlich ein Wortspiel zu „Necromancer“ (dt. Nekromant), was Geisterbeschwörer bedeutet, und „neu*ro“, also zum Nervensystem gehörend. Der Neuromancer ist ein zeitgenössischer (oder in diesem Fall in der nahen Zukunft angesiedelter) Zauberer, dessen Hexerei darin besteht, sein protoplasmisches Nervensystem mit dem elektronischen Nervensystem der Computerwelt zu interfizieren und imagistisch zu manipulieren (und selbst * Interface: unmittelbare wechselseitige Verbindung, direkter Kontakt – Der Übersetzer).
---CHUNK-SEPARATOR---
Being manipulated (like the traditional shaman who enters a trance state under the influence of drugs or other mystical means) to interact with traditional mystical realms is not new in science fiction. Similar themes can be found in my "Riding the Torch", Vernor Vinge's "True Namer", Alfred Bester's "Golem 100", and countless recent stories and novels where human protagonists act within a kind of "cyberspace", a cybernetic space. The Disney Studios even made a film based on this concept, titled "Tron". What is new and aesthetically significant about William Gibson's "Neuromancer" is that its protagonist is not a computer freak or hacker, but a cyberpunk—a term he himself coined. While some hard science fiction authors criticize Gibson for not always adhering to the scientific rigor of his work, they confirm, by their very criticism, that "Neuromancer" is indeed a work of hard science fiction in terms of its content. What really bothers them, however, beyond the scientific disagreements, is that stylistically and characterologically, "Neuromancer" bears no resemblance to previous hard science fiction novels. This seems to also irk those with a more literary inclination for similar reasons. Gibson writes hard science fiction, but not in the style of Heinlein, Anderson, Clement, or Gregory Benford. Rather, he aligns himself stylistically, philosophically, aesthetically, and in terms of his protagonist's consciousness with Ellison, William Burroughs, Michael Moorcock (creator of the Jerry Cornelius stories), and the spirit of "The Big Flash", "Bug Jack Barron" or "Street Meat". "Neuromancer" combines New Wave and hard science fiction, despite appearing contradictory. It is neuromantic, neuro-romantic, and even new romantic. When Bob Dylan performed at the Newport Folk Festival in the early 1960s with an electric guitar, the Beats, Bohemians, and folkies were outraged. This was a folk festival; the attendees were intellectuals, Dylan was their hero, and his lyrics expressed what would later become the spirit of counterculture through instrumentation. But the electric guitar was the instrument of rock 'n' roll—the music of bald-headed guys in black leather jackets, the rockers, skinheads, not mods who emerged from the counterculture. It's hard to believe today that rock once contrasted with the intellectual, cultural, aesthetic, and societal values of the elite bohemian class; that it was supposed to transform into the shared values of an entire generation through the influence of Dylan and the Beatles! And this strange contradiction persisted at the core of counterculture throughout the 1960s and well into the 1970s: their anti-technological ideology, neo-Luddism—dislike for the institutions of science and technology; bucolic mysticism; and an environmental consciousness. Rock 'n' roll, a musical style characterized by electric guitars and entirely electronic synthesizers, was a phenomenon of counterculture. The New Wave in science fiction was also, to some extent, a manifestation of counterculture, and the intense conflict between New Wavers and the old guard reflected, at a literary level, the polarization and generation conflict within society as a whole. We all know this story. The New Wave advocated for sexual liberation and cultural pluralism, while the old guard stood for traditional moral values. New Wave embraced stylistic experimentation, while the old guard favored simple, transparent storytelling. The New Wave was anti-establishment and anti-war, while the old guard supported America's troops in Vietnam. And the old guard constantly accused the New Wave of nihilism towards science and technology, while the New Wave ridiculed the old guard for their naive belief that advancements in science and technology would automatically improve human conditions. This political polarization led to a false dichotomy between science and spirit, between "hard" science fiction and stylistic experimentation, between logical positivism and "healthy" popular sentiment, between a strictly scientifically-oriented and one based on characterological realism science fiction, between computer hackers and hippies, between the scientific worldview and romantic impulses. It took a long time for us to realize that this dichotomy is illusory. We should have seen or heard it from the beginning, because the expression of romantic impulses through "high tech" instruments is at the heart of rock 'n' roll. Rock has always been the music of libidinous anarchism and romantic and transcendental impulses; without this "message", it's not really rock 'n' roll. Of course, rock has always been defined as a technological music because without electric guitars and synthesizers, it's not rock 'n' roll. The ideological politics of the 1960s and early 1970s obscured this obvious truth, clouded our vision, and hid "high tech" romance and cyborg transcendentalism. On one side were long natural hair, ecology, naturally produced food, bucolic return to nature-romanticism, Eastern mysticism, and personal fulfillment. On the other side was hard, morally justified, militaristic, rational science and technology, and a technocratic America. But in the mid-1970s, new punks rebelled against the anti-artificial, anti-technological aesthetics of counterculture. Earthy colors were out; black shiny leather and highly polished "high tech" chrome were in. Pink nickel-framed glasses were out; tinted, mirrored glasses were in. Long natural hair was out; bold, spiky, brightly colored, over-the-top hairstyles were in. A new "high tech" romance. The new punks are not technocratic slaves, but anarchistic rockers in the old romantic tradition. But they are rockers who embrace technology, just as their predecessors embraced freedom.

Being manipulated (as traditional shamans are by drugs or trance to enter into interaction with traditional mystical realms) is, as an idea in science fiction, nothing new. Similar things can be found in my "Riding the Torch", Vernor Vinge's "True Namer", Alfred Bester's "Golem 100", and countless recent stories and novels where human protagonists act within a kind of "cyberspace", a cybernetic space. The Disney Studios even made a film brimming with special effects based on it: Tron. However, what is new and aesthetically significant about Gibson's Neuromancer is that its protagonist is not a computer freak or hacker, but a computer punk - a cyberpunk if you will. While some "hard SF" authors criticize Gibson for not always meeting the scientific standards of computing, they thereby confirm that they are making an effort to critique the novel, confirming that Neuromancer, in terms of its content, is a work of "hard science fiction". But what really bothers them - apart from scientific differences - about this book is that Neuromancer stylistically and characterologically does not resemble any previous "hard SF" novel. And that seems to bother the more literary types for corresponding reasons. Gibson writes "hard science fiction", but he doesn't write it like Heinlein, Poul Anderson, Hal Clement, or Gregory Benford. In terms of style, philosophy, aesthetics, and in the conscious nature of his protagonist, Gibson is closer to Ellison, William Burroughs, Michael Moorcock of the "Jerry Cornelius" stories, and - hm! - Philip K. Dick's "The Spinrad" from "The Big Flash", "Bug Jack Barron", or "Street Meat". Neuromancer combines "New Wave" and "hard science fiction", even if it seems contradictory. Neuromantic. Neuro-romantic. But also new romantic. When Bob Dylan performed at the Newport Folk Festival in the early 1960s playing an electric guitar, the Beats, Bohemians, and folkies were outraged. This was a folk festival, the people were intellectuals, Dylan was their hero, and his lyrics expressed what would later become the spirit of counterculture through instrumentation. But the electric guitar was the instrument of rock 'n' roll - the music of bald-headed types in black leather jackets, the rockers, skinheads, not mods who emerged from counterculture. Well, kids, it's hard to believe nowadays that rock once contrasted with the intellectual, cultural, aesthetic, and societal values of the elite bohemian, that rock, after being significantly reshaped by Dylan and the Beatles, was supposed to transform into the shared values of an entire generation in the form of counterculture! And this strange contradiction persisted at the core of counterculture throughout the 1960s and well into the 1970s; its anti-technological ideology - neo-Luddism, suspicion towards the institutions of science and technology, bucolic mysticism, ecological awareness - was expressed through rock 'n' roll, a musical style characterized by electric guitars and entirely electronic synthesizers. The New Wave science fiction was, in a sense, also a phenomenon of counterculture, and the intense conflict between New Wave and the old SF guard reflected on a literary level the polarity and generation conflict in society as a whole. We all know this story. The New Wave advocated for sexual liberation and cultural pluralism, while the old guard stood for traditional moral values. New Wave was about stylistic experiments, while the old guard favored simple, transparent storytelling. The New Wave was anti-establishment and anti-war, while the old guard supported our boys in Vietnam. And the old guard constantly accused the New Wave of being nihilistic towards science and technology, while the New Wave ridiculed the old guard for their naive belief that a surge in science and technology would per se improve human conditions. Meanwhile, the political polarization at the time led to the completely false notion that there exists an irreconcilable dichotomy between science and spirit, between "hard" science fiction and stylistic experiment, between logical positivism and "healthy" popular sentiment, between strictly scientifically-oriented and characterologically-based science fiction, between computer hackers and hippies, between the scientific worldview and romantic impulse. It took a long time for us to gradually understand that this dichotomy is illusory. We should have seen or heard it from the beginning, because the expression of romantic impulses through "high tech" instruments is the heart of rock 'n' roll. Rock has always been the music of libidinal anarchism and romantic and transcendental impulses; without this "message", it's just not rock 'n' roll. Of course, rock has always been defined as a technological music, because without electric guitars and synthesizers, it's not rock 'n' roll. The ideological politics of the 1960s and early 1970s obscured this obvious truth, clouded our vision for it, and hid "high tech" romance and cyborg transcendence. On one side were long natural hair, ecology, naturally produced food, bucolic return to nature-romanticism, Eastern mysticism, and personal fulfillment. On the other side was hard, morally justified, militaristic, rational domain of science and technology, and a technocratic America. But in the mid-1970s, new punks rebelled against the anti-artificial, anti-technical aesthetics of counterculture. Flowered earth tones were out, black shiny leather and high-gloss polished "high tech" chrome were in. Out were rosy nickel-framed glasses, in were tinted, reflective lenses. Out was long natural hair, in was audacious, spiky, brightly colored, over-the-top hairstyles. A new "high tech" romance. The new punks are not technocratic slaves, but anarchist rockers in the old romantic tradition. But they are rockers who embrace the true spirit of rock 'n' roll.

Text to translate:
In this science and technology-dominated world, one readily accepts the reality of the last quarter of the twentieth century with its future prospects. So do the Neuromantics. People like Gibson and, in particular, John Shirley are Cyberpunks in as much as their work is shaped by the trappings of contemporary rocker style. Others such as Bruce Sterling, Lewis Shiner, and Greg Bear (who was quite surprised to find himself back on a Cyberpunk forum in Austin) write stories set in an environment that lacks any punk aesthetic. Gibson, and even more so Sterling and Bear, conduct scientific extrapolations just as thoroughly as established "hard science" authors like Larry Niven or Hal Clement and others. But somehow none of the Neuromantics seem to regard scientific speculation as the be-all and end-all of their work and its core theme. While only a few strive for the characteristic sharpness of Gibson's prose, they all focus directly on character, with an intensity and subtlety that clashes with the essence of traditional "hard science fiction". Nevertheless, the Neuromantics write "hard science fiction" according to any positive definition of the term and also character studies. Indeed, what unites them all is their thematic approach, both in "hard science fiction" and in character-oriented SF, and thus, the fundamental theme of any truly challenging SF: our increasingly intimate feedback relationship with the Technosphere we have created has changed, and will continue to change, what it means to be human.

John Shirley's latest novel, Eclipse, is more politically charged than any other SF novel in the last fifteen years. Set at the beginning of the next century, it employs multiple perspectives to tell the complex story of a complex resistance against a complex fascist conspiracy aiming to control the United States and Western Europe. In recent years, Shirley lived in Europe for a while and wrote political thrillers under different names, as is evident in Eclipse. One may not accept Shirley's politics without contradiction, but no SF author has confronted international political terrorism with such realism as Shirley; after all, future politics cannot be portrayed any more realistically. Although Shirley does not name anyone, the currents and conspiracies in Eclipse are undeniably derived from existing trends.

Eclipse is what is so rare today: a furious, politically engaged SF novel, something Shirley makes no secret of. What makes Shirley a true Neuromantic? Well, for one thing, most of the characters in Eclipse, at least the sympathetic ones, come from the same borderline area under the belt line of "high tech" society as in Neuromancer, and Shirley's stylistic sensibility is overall more consciously rooted in street consciousness than Gibson's. However, here the "punk feeling" is applied to a very profound and significant political analysis, so that if we accept politology as a science, we must recognize Eclipse as "hard science fiction" at its extreme.

While Eclipse is packed with conventional aggression and action, the decisive battle takes place in the Grid, as Shirley calls the international media network that covers the Earth. The fascists make sophisticated use of it, while the so-called civilized society cannot accept it. And the resistance tries desperately to gain access. What is perceived through enhanced electronic senses has more psychological reality than actual events in the so-called real world and thus shapes political reality more reliably than physical force.

At the climax of Eclipse (yes, it's the first book in a trilogy), the fascist moloch mercilessly crushes the Arc de Triomphe, the very symbol of resistance. But Rickenharp, a rock musician and resistance fighter, has filled the arch with instruments, microphones, and amplifiers, and his final performance to the world via the Grid turns the fascist victory into a symbolic glorification of resistance, which Shirley convincingly demonstrates.

This is about as romantic as the Neuromantics, or rather, Neo-Romantics, can get, and it's only fitting that the technical instruments of this victory over raw, physical reality are microphones, amplifiers, and electric guitars—the essence of rock 'n' roll.

Eclipse may be more of a Cyberpunk than a genuine Neuromantic novel because the "hard science" material is only peripherally addressed, but the absolutely central theme from rock 'n' roll to the climax is characteristic of an original Neuromantic sensibility. For Rickenharp's triumph at the end is a cyborg triumph made possible only through electronic amplification of his fleshly musicality, clearly demonstrating that cyborgs, romantic cyborgs, Neo-Romantic cyborgs, use technological enhancement for transcendental purposes since the day Dylan picked up the electric guitar.

As for typical music of our time, we have all accepted Neuromancy as given for a quarter century. And Eclipse goes even further. Rickenharp consumes a drug that enhances his musical creativity, albeit at physical cost. A red thread runs through the entire book: the connection between electronic amplification and transformation in chemically-stimulated humans, showing us what we already know: chemical consciousness alteration is also part of technology; it has long penetrated our whole society, and it's no coincidence that drugs and rock are so closely intertwined. Electronic amplification and consciousness-altering drugs have changed the parameters of human sensing and thus the definition of human perception and psyche.

Other Neuromantics take technological changes in our definitions of humanity even further. It is, after all, the acceptance of technological evolution and change in our conception of humanity—the romantic acceptance of technological transformation of the species—and not traditional mistrust against it that ultimately determines the Neuromantic sensibility.

Greg Bear, who pondered what a decent "hard science" type like himself would have lost among the Cyberpunks on that forum in Austin, unwittingly answered his own question when he left all present stunned with the most radical Neo-Romantic statement of the entire event.
